In Lake Turkana, water level fluctuations took place between 8,500 and 4,500 years before present, with highstands before 8,400, around 7,000, and between 5,500 and 5,000[729] and lowstands around 8,000, 10,000 and 12,000 years before present.[730] In total, five[731] or six separate highstands are recorded in desert varnish around the lake,[732] matching with phases of increased wetness in the Negev.[733] The highstands appear to be controlled by sea surface temperature patterns in the Atlantic and Indian Oceans, but also by overflow of water from Lake Suguta[729] and Chew Bahir and upstream lakes[734] into Lake Turkana.[735] Volcanic and tectonic phenomena occur at Lake Turkana, but do not have the magnitude required to explain large changes in lake level.[736] Water level fluctuations have also been inferred for Lake Chad on the basis of pollen data, especially towards the end of the AHP.[737] In the Taoudenni lake fluctuations of about a quarter-millennium have been recorded[738] and frequent droughts occurred in the Eastern Sahara.[739]
Other variations appear to have occurred 9,500–9,000 and 7,400–6,800[302] as well as 10,200, 8,200, 6,600 and 6,000 years before present; they were accompanied by decreased population density in parts of the Sahara,[724] and other dry interludes in Egypt have been noted 9,400–9,300, 8,800–8,600, 7,100–6,900 and 6,100–5,900 years ago.[740] The duration and severity of dry events are difficult to reconstruct,[384] and the impact of events such as the Younger Dryas is heterogeneous even across neighbouring areas.[741] During dry episodes, humans might have headed to waterbodies which still had resources,[401] and cultural changes in the central Sahara have been linked to some dry episodes.[742] Aside from fluctuations, a decrease in Nile discharge[743] and southward retreat of the humid period may have been underway after 8,000 years ago[744] with a major drought around 7,800 years ago.[745]
End
[edit]
The African humid period ended about 6,000–5,000 years ago;[746] an ending date of 5,500 years before present is often used.[747] After vegetation declined,[205] the Sahara became barren and was claimed by sand.[135] Wind erosion increased in northern Africa,[748] and dust export from the now-desert[724] and from dried up lakes[749] such as the Bodélé Basin grew; Bodélé today is the largest single source of dust on Earth.[750] The lakes dried up, mesic vegetation disappeared, and sedentary human populations were replaced by more mobile cultures.[21] The transition from the "green Sahara" to the present-day dry Sahara is considered to be the greatest environmental transition of the Holocene in northern Africa;[751] today almost no precipitation falls in the region.[50] The end of the AHP but also its beginning could be considered a "climate crisis" given the strong and extended impact.[714] Drying extended as far as the Azores,[752] Canary Islands[753] and southeastern Iran,[754] and there is evidence of climate change on São Nicolau, Cape Verde.[755]
The Piora Oscillation cold period in the Alps[756] coincides with the end of the AHP;[413] the period 5,600–5,000 years ago was characterized by widespread cooling and more variable precipitation changes around the world[757] and was possibly forced by changes in solar activity and orbital parameters.[758] It has also been named "Mid-Holocene Transition".[759] Some changes in climate possibly extended into southeastern Australia,[760] Central America[761] and into South America.[762] The neoglacial began.[763] In the Chew Bahir basin, several short droughts may have "heralded" the end of the AHP; such short-term climatic fluctuations are common before a major climatic change.[375]
A major pan-tropical environmental change took place about 4,000 years ago.[764] This change was accompanied by the collapse of ancient civilizations, severe drought in Africa, Asia, and the Middle East, and the retreat of glaciers on Mount Kilimanjaro[765] and Mount Kenya.[766]
Chronology
[edit]
Whether the drying happened everywhere at the same time and whether it took place in centuries or millennia is unclear[223] in part due to disagreeing records[260] and has led to controversy,[239] and such a disagreement on timing also exists with respect to the expected vegetation changes.[220] Marine cores usually indicates an abrupt change[767] but not without exceptions[57] while pollen data do not, perhaps due to regional and local differences in vegetation.[768] Africa is a diverse landscape[769] and groundwater and local vegetation can modify local conditions;[359] groundwater-fed water bodies, for example, persisted longer than those nourished by rain.[273] The debate on how quickly the Sahara formed goes back to 1849, when the Prussian naturalist Alexander von Humboldt suggested that only a quick drying could form the desert.[770]
In the 2010s, the idea has taken hold that the end of the African humid period occurred in a stepwise fashion from north to south.[771] In northeastern Asia,[772] the western Sahara and east Africa, the humid period ended within 500 years[773] with a one-step drying 6,000 – 5,000 years ago north of the present-day monsoon belt. Farther south, precipitation decrease was more protracted[774] and closer to the equator, the AHP ended between 4,000[110] and 2,500 years ago.[18] In East Africa, pronounced drying occurred between 4,500 and 3,500 years ago, centered on 4,000 years ago;[237] Egypt during the Old Kingdom was still wetter than today.[775] A later end in northeast Africa about 4,000 years ago may reflect the different configuration of landmasses and thus monsoon behaviour,[776] while other research has found a westward propagating drying trend.[109] An earlier end by 6,100 years ago has also been suggested.[777]
Some evidence points to a two-phase change in climate, with two distinct dry transitions[778] caused by two distinct steps in the decrease in insolation at which climate changes.[779] Distinct environmental changes may have occurred in Central, Western, and Eastern Africa.[239] In Asia, an abrupt drying has been noted in numerous Chinese lakes.[780] Finally, sometimes the 4.2 kiloyear event – the transition from the Northgrippian to the Meghalayan stage of the Holocene – [717] is considered to be the true end of the AHP,[722] especially in central Africa.[781]
Increased variability in precipitation may have preceded the end of the AHP; this pattern is commonly observed before a sudden climate change.[782] In Gilf Kebir, between 6,300 and 5,200 years ago apparently a winter rainfall regime became established as the AHP ended.[188] Later fluctuations in climate that produced brief humid spells also took place,[783] such as moister periods 2,100 years before present in the western Sahel,[111] between 2,200 - 1,500 years ago in Ethiopia[125] and between 500 BCE – 300 CE in Roman Northern Africa and along the Dead Sea.[784] By 2,700 years ago, the central Sahara had become a desert and remained one until the present day.[785]
Sahara and Sahel
[edit]
After a first brief lake level drop[786] between 5,700 and 4,700 calibrated years ago that might reflect climate variability towards the end of the African humid period,[787] water levels in Lake Megachad decreased quickly after 5,200 years before present.[788] It shrank to about 5% of its former size,[289] with the deeper northern Bodele basin drying up entirely about 2,000[296]–1,000 years ago[789] as it was disconnected from the southern basin where its major tributary, the Chari River, enters Lake Chad.[289] The dried out basin was now exposed to the Harmattan winds, which blow dust out of the dry lake bed,[790] making it the single largest source of dust in the world.[791] Dunes formed in the dried-up Sahara[792] and Sahel[793] or began moving again after stabilizing during the AHP.[794]
The tropical vegetation was replaced by desert vegetation, in some places suddenly and in others more gradually.[795] Along the Atlantic coast, the vegetation retreat was slowed by a stage of sea level rise that increased soil moisture levels, delaying the retreat by about two millennia.[796] A gradual decline has been noted in the Tibesti.[797] In Libya at Wadi Tanezzuft, the end of the humid period was also delayed by leftover water in dune systems and in the Tassili mountains until 2,700 years ago, when river activity finally ceased.[798] A brief moist pulse between 5,000 – 4,000 years ago in the Tibesti led to the development of the so-called "Lower Terrace".[799] The Egyptian Sahara might still have been vegetated until 4,200 years ago, based on depictions of savanna environments in Fifth Dynasty tombs in Egypt.[800]
At Lake Yoa, which is groundwater-fed,[801] vegetation decreased and the desert took over between 4,700–4,300 and 2,700 years ago,[802] while the lake became hypersaline 4,000 years ago.[803] Lake Teli dried out completely about 4,200 years ago.[804] However, the climate of the Ounianga lakes may have been affected by the Tibesti Mountains and the end of the AHP, thus delayed,[788] and fossil groundwater left by the AHP nourishes the lake to this day.[805] In the central Sahara, water resources in the mountains persisted longer.[806]
East Africa and Arabia
[edit]
In northern East Africa, water levels dropped rapidly about 5,500 years ago[201], while in Hoti Cave in Arabia, the Indian Monsoon retreated southward about 5,900 years ago.[113] Drying is also documented from Oman,[120] and rivers and lakes of Arabia became intermittent or entirely dry.[807] The Blue Nile basin became less moist[120] with a noticeable decrease of Nile discharge about 4,000 years ago.[608] Decreased discharge of the Nile led to the cessation of sapropel deposition and turbidite activity off its delta,[103] the concentration/[808] abandonment of river channels in its delta and upstream[809] and increased seawater influence in the delta.[810]
Reconstructions from Lake Abiyata in Ethiopia suggest that the end of the African humid period took the form of severe droughts rather than a gradual decrease of precipitation.[811] Drying in Arabia commenced about 7,000 calibrated years ago[462] and there are large disparities in the timing between various parts of Arabia[812] but a tendency towards an arid climate between 7,000 and 5,000 years ago has been observed[813] which continued until 2,700 years ago.[814] In the Bale Mountains and the Sanetti Plateau of Ethiopia, vegetation changes signalling a drier climate occurred around 4,600 years ago.[815]
Forest cover in the area of the African Great Lakes decreased between 4,700 and 3,700 years ago,[553] although drying at Lake Rukwa began 6,700 years ago[816] and transition to saline conditions took place 5,500 years ago.[549] At Lake Edward, major changes in lake chemistry consistent with drying are noted 5,200 years ago. There was a minor recovery in vegetation between 2,500 and 2,000 years ago, followed by a much more rapid appearance of grasses, accompanied by substantial wildfire activity. This might have been the most severe drought in the Lake Edward region during the Holocene, with many lakes, such as Lake George, dropping significantly or drying up altogether.[817] Other lakes such as Nakuru, Turkana, Lake Chew Bahir, Lake Abbe and Lake Zway also dropped between 5,400 and 4,200 years ago.[818] Decreased vegetation cover in the catchment of the Blue Nile has been correlated with increased sediment transport in the river beginning 3,600 – 4,000 years ago.[819] 5,000 years ago, Lake Nabugabo separated from Lake Victoria owing to decreasing water levels.[820] The expansion of savanna in East Africa has been linked to the end of the AHP.[821]
The end of the AHP at Lake Turkana occurred about 5,000[731]–5,300 years before present, accompanied by a lake level decline[822] and the cessation of overflow from other lakes in its area into Lake Turkana.[503] Between 5,000 and 4,200, Lake Turkana became more saline; its water levels decreased below the level of outflow to the Nile.[823] Towards the end of the AHP water temperatures in the lake and in other regional lakes appear to have increased, followed by a drop after its end[824] possibly resulting from the insolation seasonality pattern that was in force at the time of the end of the AHP.[825] The decrease in water level was accompanied by a decrease in sedimentation rates,[826] and there is evidence that faulting and volcanism may have increased at Turkana due to the water level drop.[827] The decrease of water levels in Lake Turkana also impacted the Nile and the Predynastic societies dependent on it.[828]
Mediterranean
[edit]
The southern Aegean,[829] Libya and the Middle Atlas became gradually more dry,[795] and drying in Morocco took place about 6,000 radiocarbon years ago,[778] Drier conditions in Iberia and the Western Mediterranean accompanied the end of the African humid period between 6,000 and 4,000 years ago, perhaps as a consequence of increasingly frequent positive North Atlantic Oscillation episodes and the shift of the ITCZ[830] More complicated changes have been found for the northern margin of the Mediterranean,[831] and winter rainfall increased in the Levant at the end of the AHP.[832] A 4.2 kiloyear event is recorded in dust records from the Mediterranean[833] and might have been caused by changes in the circulation of the Atlantic Ocean.[180]
Tropical West Africa
[edit]
In Lake Bosumtwi, the African humid period ended about 3,000 years ago[135] after a brief moistening between 5,410 ± 80 years ago that ended 3,170 ± 70 years ago. These earlier, similar changes in Western Senegal and later, similar changes in the Congo Fan appear to reflect a southward shift of the precipitation zone over time.[723] Some drying occurred simultaneously between the Sahel and the Gulf of Guinea.[208] Some lakes in the Guineo-Congolian region dried out, while others were relatively unaffected.[796]
A general tendency towards a drier climate is observed in West Africa at the end of the AHP.[834] There, dense vegetation thinned progressively between 5,000 and 3,000 years ago,[817] and major perturbations in the vegetation occurred between 4,200 and 2,400 years ago.[835] A brief return of moister conditions took place 4,000 years ago[714] while a substantial dry phase occurred between 3,500 and 1,700 years ago.[834] Aridity became established between 5,200 and 3,600 years ago in the Sahara.[836] In Senegal mangroves collapsed 2,500 years ago,[212] and modern-type vegetation arose about 2,000 years ago,[837] aided by a decrease of sea level after the middle Holocene.[838]
Central Africa
[edit]
Farther south at the equator between 6,100 and 3,000 calibrated years before present savannah expanded at the expense of forests, with the transition possibly lasting until 2,500 calibrated years before present;[764] a different time course estimate for the area between 4° southern and 7° northern latitude states that forest cover decreased between 4,500 and 1,300 years ago.[796] In the Adamawa Plateau (Cameroon[839]), the Ubangui Plateau (Central African Republic[839]) and the Cameroon Volcanic Line montane forests disappeared at the end of the African humid period.[840] In the Adamawa Plateau savanna has continuously expanded since 4,000 calibrated years ago.[841] Such a change took also place in Benin and Nigeria between 4,500 and 3,400 calibrated years ago.[796] Climate around the Gulf of Guinea became drier at the end of the AHP, although forests remained stable on Sao Tome.[577] In the Congo Basin, there were changes in the composition and density of the forests rather than their extent,[842] and along the equator precipitation may have increased around 4.2 ka.[843] Many vegetation changes in the tropical regions were probably caused by a longer dry season[844] and perhaps a smaller latitudinal range of the ITCZ.[841]
Southern Hemisphere Africa
[edit]
In the Southern Hemisphere, at Lake Malawi, drying began later – 1,000 years before present – as did the African humid period, which there began only about 8,000 years ago.[824] Contrarily, increased water levels in Etosha Pan (Namibia) appear to relate to a southward movement of the ITCZ at the end of the AHP[845] although stalagmite growth data in Dante Cave also in Namibia has been interpreted as indicating a wetter climate during the AHP.[846] Several records indicate that 5,500 years ago, precipitation changed in an east-west dipole-like way[847] with drying in the west and moistening in the east.[848] This pattern was probably driven by shifts in atmospheric moisture transport and of rain belt width.[849]
Mechanisms
[edit]
The end of the humid period appears to reflect changes in insolation during the Holocene,[110] as a progressive decrease in summer insolation reduced the insolation gradients between Earth's hemispheres.[850] However, the drying appears to have been much more abrupt than the insolation changes;[132] it is not clear whether non-linear feedbacks led to abrupt changes in climate; it is also unclear whether the process, driven by orbital changes, was abrupt.[135] Also, the Southern Hemisphere warmed, and this resulted in a southward shift of the ITCZ;[851] orbitally-driven insolation has increased over the Holocene in the Southern Hemisphere.[124]
As precipitation decreased, so did vegetation, in turn increasing the albedo and further decreasing precipitation.[139] Furthermore, vegetation may have responded to increased variations in precipitation towards the end of the AHP,[136] although this view has been challenged.[852] This could have directed sudden changes in precipitation, although this view has been cast in doubt by the observation that in many places the end of the African humid period was gradual rather than sudden.[853] Plants at higher and lower latitudes might respond differently to climate change; for example, more diverse plant communities might have slowed down the end of the AHP.[81]
Other proposed mechanisms:
- Decreases in polar insolation through altered cosmic ray fluxes might promote the growth of sea ice and cooling at high latitudes, which in turn result in stronger equator-to-pole temperature gradients, stronger subtropical anticyclones and more intense upwelling in for example the Benguela current.[199]
- Changes in the circulation of high latitude oceans may have played a role,[850] such as the potential occurrence of another meltwater/ice rafting pulse around 5,700 years before present.[851] The decreased insolation during the mid-Holocene may have made the climate system more sensitive to changes, explaining why earlier comparable pulses did not terminate the humid period for good.[854]
- There is evidence that glaciers in Tibet such as at Nanga Parbat expanded during the Holocene, especially towards the end of the AHP.[855] In climate models, increased snow and ice on the Tibetan Plateau can lead to a weakening of the Indian and African monsoons, with the weakening of the former preceding that of the latter by 1,500–2,000 years.[856]
- Decreases in sea surface temperatures of the Indian Ocean may be involved in the drying of East Africa, but there is no agreement on the temperature records from that ocean.[171] Moreover, there is no evidence of temperature changes in the Gulf of Guinea at the critical time that might explain the end of the AHP.[201]
- Additional feedback processes may have included the drying of soils and loss of vegetation after decreased rainfall,[135] which would have led to wind-driven deflation of the soils.[857]
- An expansion of sea ice around Greenland, Ellesmere Island 6,000[858] and Antarctica about 5,000 calibrated years ago may have provided another positive feedback.[859]
- The expanding dry belt of the Sahara pushed the regions of cyclogenesis in the Mediterranean northwest-northward, resulting in wind changes[860] and precipitation regime changes in parts of Italy.[861]
- Climate change at high latitudes has been proposed as a cause for the end of the AHP. Specifically, about 6,000–5,000 years ago the Arctic became colder, with sea ice expanding, temperatures in Europe and off Northern Africa decreasing and the Atlantic meridional overturning circulation weakening.[201] This cooling tendency may have weakened the Tropical Easterly Jet and thus reduced the amount of precipitation falling over Africa.[862]
The orbitally-induced changes of precipitation may have been modified by the solar cycle; specifically, solar activity maxima during the ending phase of the AHP may have offset the orbital effect and thus stabilized precipitation levels, while solar activity minima compounded the orbital effects and thus induced rapid decreases in water levels of Lake Turkana.[863] At Lake Victoria, on the other hand, solar variations appear to sometimes lead to drought and sometimes lead to wetness, probably due to changes in the ITCZ.[851]
Potentially human-mediated changes
[edit]
Major changes in vegetation in East Africa about 2,000 years ago may have been caused by human activity, including large-scale deforestation for iron production during the Iron Age.[864] Similar changes have been observed on the Adamawa Plateau[865] (Cameroon[839]), but later dating of archaeological sites has found no correlation between human expansion in Cameroon and environmental degradation.[866] Similar rainforest degradation across Western African took place between 3,000 and 2,000 years ago[867] and the degradation is also known as "third millennium rainforest crisis".[868] Climate-mediated processes may have increased the impact of land use changes in East Africa.[556] In the Sudanian and Sahelian savannah, on the other hand, human activity seems to have had little impact,[289] and in Central Africa, forest changes were clearly triggered by climate change with little or no evidence of anthropogenic changes.[869] The question has led to intense debate among paleoecologists and archaeologists.[870]
While humans were active in Africa during the end of the African humid period, climate models analyzed by Claussen and colleagues 1999 indicate that its end does not need any human activity as an explanation,[871] although vegetation changes may have been induced by human activity[240] and grazing.[872] Later it was suggested that overgrazing may have triggered the end of the AHP around 5,500 years ago;[359] human influence might explain why the Sahara became a desert without the accompanying onset of an ice age; usually the existence of a Sahara desert is associated with the expansion of high latitude glaciers.[420] Later research has on the contrary suggested that human pastoralism may have actually delayed the end of the AHP by half a millennium[873] as moving herds of animals driven by humans seeking good pasture conditions may lead to more balanced impacts of pastures on the vegetation and thus to greater vegetation quality.[874] Which effects prevailed is still controversial.[387] Increased grazing has been invoked to explain the increase in dust emissions after the end of the AHP.[875] The effects of grazing on vegetation cover are context-dependent and hard to generalize over wider regions.[876]
Global
[edit]
A general drying trend is observed in the northern tropics,[877] and between 5,000 and 4,500 calibrated years ago, the monsoons weakened.[878] Perhaps as a consequence of the end of the AHP,[879] Asian monsoon precipitation declined between 5,000 and 4,000 years ago.[31] A drought 5,500 years ago is recorded in Mongolia[880] and eastern America, where drought conditions around 5,500–5,000 years ago occurred in places like Florida[881] and between New Hampshire and Ontario.[882] A drying tendency is also noted in the Caribbean and the Central Atlantic.[883] The final retreat of vegetation from the Sahara may have helped cause the 4.2 kiloyear event.[884]
Conversely, in South America there is evidence that the monsoon behaves oppositely consistent with precessional forcing;[877] water levels in Lake Titicaca were low during the middle Holocene and began to rise again after the end of the AHP.[885] Likewise, a trend towards increased wetness took place in the Rocky Mountains at this time[886] although it was accompanied by a drier phase around Lake Tahoe, California and in the Western United States.[887] Widespread climate change occurred around the North Atlantic at the time the AHP ended, and there are connections between the North American and African climate.[888] The end of the AHP may have reduced heat transport into the Arctic, causing cooling there.[889]
Consequences
[edit]
Humans
[edit]
As observed at archaeological sites, settlement activity in the Sahara decreased after the AHP.[890] Beginning from the north,[891] population in Northern Africa decreased between 6,300 - 5,200[365] or 5,300 years ago,[256] taking less than a millennium.[857] In inner Arabia, many settlements were abandoned about 5,300 years ago[143] and there was a transition in Arabian monument construction.[892] Some Neolithic people in the desert persisted for longer thanks to the exploitation of groundwater.[778]
Different human populations responded to the drying in diverse ways,[403] with responses in Western Sahara distinct from those in Central Sahara.[10] In the Sahara, subsistence[893] and pastoralism replaced hunter-gatherer activity[894] and a more nomadic lifestyle replaced semi-sedentary lifestyles[895] as observed in the Acacus Mountains of Libya.[381] Nomadic lifestyles also developed in the Eastern Sahara/Red Sea Hills in response to the end of the AHP.[896] There was a shift in domestic animal use from cattle to sheep and goats as these are more suited in arid climates, a change reflected in rock art from which cattle disappeared at this time.[897]
The development of irrigation systems in Arabia may have been an adaptation to the drying tendency.[462] The decreased availability of resources forced human populations to adapt;[898] in general, fishing and hunting declined in favour of farming and herding.[899] However, the effects of the end of the AHP on human food production have been subject to controversy.[900]
The warm episode and coinciding drought may have triggered animal and human migration to less inhospitable areas[825] and the appearance of pastoralists where previously fishery-dependent societies had existed, as happened at Lake Turkana.[509] Humans moved to the Nile,[ad] where the society of Ancient Egypt with pharaohs and pyramids was eventually forged by these climate refugees[857] perhaps reflecting renewed exuberance;[413] thus the end of the AHP can be considered responsible for the birth of Ancient Egypt.[904] Lower water levels in the Nile also aided the settlement of its valley as has been observed at Kerma.[905] A similar process may have led to the development of the Garamantian civilization.[906] Such human migrations towards more hospitable conditions along rivers and the development of irrigation also took place along the Euphrates, Tigris and Indus, leading to the development of the Sumerian and Harappan civilizations.[81] During the so-called "Dark Millennium" between 6,000–5,000 years ago, people left the southern coast of the Persian Gulf for more hospitable areas in what is present-day Oman.[907] Population shifts into mountain areas have also been reported for the Air Mountains, Hoggar and Tibesti.[640] In other places, such as the Acacus Mountains, populations conversely remained in oases[908] and hunter-gatherers also stayed in the Horn of Africa.[909]
The Nile itself was not totally unaffected however;[498] the 4.2 kiloyear event[910] and the end of the AHP may be linked to the collapse of the Old Kingdom in Egypt[223] when the Nile floods failed for three decades around 4,160 years before present[911] and the final drying occurred.[912] The ongoing decrease of precipitation after the end of the AHP could be the cause of the end of the Akkadian Kingdom in Mesopotamia.[913] The end of the Garamantian civilization may also relate to climate change although other historical events were probably more important;[914] at Tanezzuft oasis after 1,600 years ago it certainly relates to the drying trend.[908]
In Central Africa, forests became discontinuous and savannahs formed in some places, facilitating the movement and growth of Bantu speaking populations;[853] these in turn may have affected the ecosystem.[915] The vegetation changes may have aided in the establishment of agriculture.[869] The relatively slow decline of precipitation gave humans more time to adapt to the changing climate conditions.[564] In East Africa, the beginning of the "Pastoral Neolithic" and the appearance of Nderit pottery have been attributed to the climatic changes at the end of the AHP.[916]
Cultural changes may also have occurred as a consequence of climate change, such as[917] changes in gender roles, the development of elites,[918] the increased presence of human burials where formerly cattle burials predominated,[919] as well as an increase of monumental architecture in the Sahara may have also been a response to increasingly adverse climates.[894] A spread in cattle domestication at the time of climate change[381] and as herders escaped the drying Sahara southwards[920] may also relate to these events, although the details of the exact process by which cattle domestication spread are still controversial.[921] Finally, changes in agricultural practices at the end of the AHP may be associated with the propagation of malaria and one of its causative pathogens Plasmodium falciparum; in turn these may correlate with the origin of human genome variants such as sickle cell disease that are linked to malaria resistance.[922]
Non-human
[edit]
In the Sahara, animal and plant populations were fragmented and restricted to certain favoured areas, such as the moist areas of mountain ranges; this happened, for example, to fish and crocodiles, which persist only in isolated water bodies. Mediterranean plants[923] such as cypresses too persist only in mountains,[924] along with some reptiles that may have also been stranded in mountains by the drying.[925] The whip spider Musicodamon atlanteus is probably also a relic of past wetter conditions.[926] The development of human-specific populations of the malaria-transmitting mosquito Aedes aegypti coincides with the end of the AHP.[927] The buffalo species Syncerus antiquus probably went extinct from the increased competition of pastoralists triggered by the climate drying.[928] Goat populations in Ethiopia shrunk during the droughts that followed the end of the AHP,[929] and lion[930] and possibly barley habitat declined across Africa.[931] The drying of the African Great Lakes region split gorilla populations into western and eastern populations,[932] and a similar population split between the parasitic wood wasp species Chalinus albitibialis and Chalinus timnaensis in Northern Africa and the Middle East may have also been caused by the expansion of deserts there.[933] Some aquatic species disappeared from the Sahara.[355] Giraffes, widespread in the Sahara during the AHP, may have been forced to migrate into the Sahel; this, together with the separating effect of Lake Megachad, may have influenced the development of giraffe subspecies.[934] Climate change together with human impacts may have led to the extinction of many large mammals in Egypt,[935] such as the hartebeest in the Sahara.[936] In the Ruwenzori Mountains, vegetation changes may have been enhanced by climatic cooling.[551] In northern Madagascar, wildlife declined after the end of the AHP even before the arrival of humans.[937] On the other hand, the decline of tree cover may have grown the niche available to domestic animals,[938] and modern Afromontane vegetation[939] and some drought-tolerant plant species may have expanded their range.[940]
The Dahomey Gap[ae] formed 4,500–3,200 years before present, correlative to the end of the AHP.[942] The harbour porpoise declined in the Mediterranean due to a switch to oligotrophic conditions as discharge from African rivers decreased.[609] Desert varnish formed on exposed rocks in the Sahara[943] and at Lake Turkana in East Africa.[731] Tectonic faulting[526] and mass wasting eroded shorelines left by the AHP.[944]
Global climate
[edit]
The shrinkage of subtropical wetlands likely led to a drop in atmospheric methane concentrations between 5,500 and 5,000 years ago, before boreal wetlands expanded and offset the loss of subtropical wetlands, resulting in a return to higher atmospheric methane concentrations.[714] Conversely, increases in atmospheric methane concentrations, detected in Greenland ice cores about 14,700 years ago,[105] and atmospheric carbon dioxide decreases in the early Holocene may relate to the vegetation expansion caused by the AHP.[945] The increase in concentration beginning 7,000 years ago may reflect increased aridity,[913] although other processes were probably more important.[946]
A sudden increase in the amount of land-originating dust in an oceanic drill core off Cape Blanc, Mauritania, has been interpreted as reflecting the end of the AHP 5,500 years ago, occurring in only a few centuries.[947] Increased African dust deposition took place at Ciomad,[948] central Portugal[949] and the Durmitor Massif, all in Europe.[950] Potentially, alluvial[af] sediments emplaced during the AHP[952] and dried up lake basins became an important source for dust[803] and silt-sized particles.[953] Today, the Sahara is the single largest source of dust in the world,[ag] with far ranging effects on climate and ecosystems,[955] such as the growth of the Amazon rainforest.[956]
In one climate model, the desertification of the Sahara at the end of the AHP reduces the amount of heat transported in the atmosphere and ocean towards the poles, inducing cooling of 1–2 °C (1.8–3.6 °F), especially in winter in the Arctic and an expansion of sea ice. Reconstructed temperatures in the Arctic indeed show a cooling, although less pronounced than in the climate model.[957] Further, this climate transition in the climate model is accompanied by increased negative Arctic Oscillation states, a weaker subpolar gyre and increased precipitation and cold air outbreaks in much of Europe; such changes have also been observed in paleoclimate data.[958] These findings imply that the vegetation state of the Sahara influences the Northern Hemisphere climate.[959] In turn, this high latitude cooling may have further reduced precipitation over Africa.[862] Hou et al. 2024 proposed that the drying of the Sahara induced drying in northern China and moistening in southern China,[960] through a cooling in the Indo-Pacific Warm Pool and an eastward shift of the Walker circulation.[961] This was accompanied by cultural changes in China, with a decline in the number of archeological sites,[962] and the Walker circulation change may be a causal mechanism for the 4.2 ka event.[963]
Present-day situation
[edit]
Presently, the African Monsoon still influences the climate between 5° south and 25° north latitude; the latitudes around 10° north receive the bulk of their precipitation from the monsoon[ah] during summer, with smaller amounts of rainfall occurring farther north. Thus, farther north deserts can be found while the moister areas are vegetated.[136] In the Central Sahara, annual precipitation reaches no more than 50–100 millimetres per year (2.0–3.9 in/year).[965] Even farther north, the desert's margin coincides with the area where the westerlies bring precipitation;[966] they also influence southernmost Africa.[967] Subsidence of air over parts of Northern Africa is responsible for the existence of deserts, which is further increased by the radiative cooling over the desert.[1] Climate variability exists to this day, with the Sahel suffering from droughts in the 1970s and 1980s when precipitation decreased by 30% and the flow of the Niger River and Senegal River even more,[968] followed by an increase of precipitation.[1] The droughts are one of the most significant climate anomalies of the 20th century.[969] Sea surface temperatures and feedbacks from land surface conditions modulate the strength of the monsoon,[970] and the droughts may have been triggered by sea surface temperature changes forced by anthropogenic aerosols.[689] A large increase in dust fluxes after 1800 AD has been explained with changed agricultural practices.[971]
In East Africa the monsoon leads to two rain seasons in the equatorial area, the so-called "long rains" in March–May and the "short rains" in October–November[972] when the ITCZ moves northward and southward over the region, respectively;[973] in addition to the Indian Ocean-sourced precipitation there is also Atlantic[ai]- and Congo-sourced precipitation west of the Congo Air Boundary.[972] In Arabia, the monsoon does not penetrate far from the Arabian Sea; some areas are under the influence of winter precipitation brought by cyclones from the Mediterranean Sea.[974] East Africa is also influenced by monsoon circulations.[975] South Africa has monsoonal, winter-precipitation, and nonseasonal precipitation climates.[976]
Implications for future global warming
[edit]
Some simulations of global warming and increased carbon dioxide concentrations have shown a substantial increase in precipitation in the Sahel/Sahara.[133] This and the increased plant growth directly induced by carbon dioxide[970] could lead to an expansion of vegetation into present-day desert, although it would be less extensive than during the mid-Holocene[133] and perhaps accompanied by a northward shift of the desert, i.e., a drying of northernmost Africa.[977] Such a precipitation increase may also reduce the amount of dust originating in Northern Africa,[978] with effects on hurricane activity in the Atlantic and increased threats of hurricane strikes in the Caribbean, the Gulf of Mexico and the East Coast of the United States.[979]
The Special Report on Global Warming of 1.5 °C and the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report indicate that global warming will likely result in increased precipitation across most of East Africa, parts of Central Africa, and the principal wet season of West Africa, although there is significant uncertainty related to these projections, especially for West Africa.[980] In addition, the end of the 20th century drying trend may be due to global warming.[981] On the other hand, West Africa[982] and parts of East Africa may become drier during certain seasons and months.[982] Currently, the Sahel is becoming greener, but precipitation has not fully recovered to levels reached in the mid-20th century.[977]
Climate models have yielded equivocal results about the effects of anthropogenic global warming on the Sahara/Sahel precipitation. Human-caused climate change occurs through different mechanisms than the natural climate change that led to the AHP:[983] Human-mediated climate change drives increased precipitation mainly through increased atmospheric moisture availability[984] and disproportionate warming of the extratropics which strengthen the monsoon circulation,[689] moderated by increased atmospheric stability, while the natural climate change is driven by a stronger monsoon circulation.[985] The direct effect of heat on plants may be detrimental.[986] Non-linear increases in vegetation cover are also possible,[689] with several climate models showing abrupt increases when global temperatures rise by 2–4 °C (3.6–7.2 °F).[987] One study in 2003 showed that vegetation intrusions in the Sahara can occur within decades after strong rises in atmospheric carbon dioxide[988] but would not cover more than about 45% of the Sahara.[54] That climate study also indicated that vegetation expansion can only occur if grazing or other perturbations to vegetation growth do not hamper it.[989] On the other hand, increased irrigation and other measures to increase vegetation growth such as the Great Green Wall could enhance it.[986] A 2022 study indicated that while increased greenhouse gas concentrations by themselves are not sufficient to start an AHP if greenhouse gas-vegetation feedbacks are ignored, they lower the threshold for orbital changes to induce Sahara greening.[990]
Plans to geoengineer the Sahara to increase its vegetation cover and precipitation have been proposed since the 19th century.[986] The mechanisms and consequences of the AHP are an important context to evaluate such proposals and their ramifications;[970] precipitation may increase,[986] but the consumption of carbon dioxide would be small; there could be detrimental impacts on climate and dust fluxes in the far-field.[991] Building the Great Green Wall[992] and large solar farms in the Sahara desert would also act to decrease its albedo and may trigger similar climate responses.[993]
A greening of the Sahara on the one hand may allow agriculture and pastoralism to expand into hitherto unsuitable areas, but increased precipitation can also lead to increased water-borne diseases and flooding.[994] Expanded human activity resulting from a wetter climate may be vulnerable to climate reversals, as demonstrated by the droughts that followed the mid-20th-century wet period.[995]
